Randomized Rank-Structured Matrix Compression by Tagging
Katherine J. Pearce, Anna Yesypenko, James Levitt, Per-Gunnar Martinsson

Abstract




Abstract

In this work, we present novel randomized compression algorithms for flat rank-structured matrices with shared bases, known as uniform Block Low-Rank (BLR) matrices. Our main contribution is a technique called tagging, which improves upon the efficiency of basis matrix computation while preserving accuracy compared to alternative methods. Tagging operates on the matrix using matrix-vector products of the matrix and its adjoint, making it particularly advantageous in scenarios where accessing individual matrix entries is computationally expensive or infeasible.

Flat rank-structured formats use subblock sizes that asymptotically scale with the matrix size to ensure competitive complexities for linear algebraic operations, making alternative methods prohibitively expensive in such scenarios. In contrast, tagging reconstructs basis matrices using a constant number of matrix-vector products followed by linear post-processing, with the constants determined by the rank parameter and the problem’s underlying geometric properties.

We provide a detailed analysis of the asymptotic complexity of tagging, demonstrating its ability to significantly reduce computational costs without sacrificing accuracy. We also establish a theoretical connection between the optimal construction of tagging matrices and projective varieties in algebraic geometry, suggesting a hybrid numeric-symbolic avenue of future work.

To validate our approach, we apply tagging to compress uniform BLR matrices arising from the discretization of integral and partial differential equations. Empirical results show that tagging outperforms alternative compression techniques, significantly reducing both the number of required matrix-vector products and overall computational time. These findings highlight the practicality and scalability of tagging as an efficient method for flat rank-structured matrices in scientific computing.

1
Introduction

In scientific computing and data science, many applications involve matrices that are dense but “data-sparse,” admitting certain low-rank approximations that compress the matrices while preserving their critical information.
Algorithms to compress data-sparse matrices can achieve better performance by invoking
rank structure
, where the input matrices are tessellated into blocks that are either small enough in size to apply dense algorithms or are of low numerical rank.
Not only can rank-structured matrices be stored and applied to vectors efficiently, but often they can also be approximately inverted or LU-factorized in linear or close-to-linear time.

There are many rank-structured matrix formats that have been successfully utilized in engineering and data science applications.
These formats are classified as either hierarchical
[
8
,
9
,
25
,
12
,
18
,
21
]
or flat
[
3
,
4
,
6
,
29
,
32
,
45
]
, corresponding to either nested or non-nested matrix tessellations, respectively.
Both hierarchical and flat rank-structured matrices are further characterized by either a weak or strong admissibility criterion.
In the weakly admissible formats, every off-diagonal block is said to be admissible, or treated as low-rank, while the admissible blocks of strongly admissible rank-structured matrices correspond only to the “far-field” of a given matrix block (as in, e.g., the Fast-Multipole Method
[
22
]
).

The last key defining feature of rank-structured matrices has historically only applied to hierarchical formats, namely whether all admissible blocks in the same block-row or block-column are well-approximated by the same low-rank basis matrices.
This property does not hold for the
\mathcal{H}
-matrix format originally proposed in
[
25
]
which necessitates that separate basis matrices be computed for each admissible block.
However, for many applications, basis matrix computations can be accelerated without significantly impacting the accuracy of the low-rank approximation by employing a shared basis assumption, where the row or column spaces of all admissible blocks within the same block-row or block-column are spanned by the same basis.
This shared basis assumption characterizes the uniform
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-matrix
[
25
,
38
]
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-matrix
[
24
,
17
]
formats in the strong admissibility setting, while the HSS
[
12
,
52
]
and HBS
[
18
,
35
]
matrix formats make use of shared bases under a weak admissibility condition.

Despite the compelling analysis
[
4
,
5
,
29
]
and broad applicability of the flat block low-rank (BLR) format
[
3
]
in areas such as sparse direct solvers
[
5
,
48
]
, modeling
[
1
,
11
]
, and boundary integral equations
[
2
]
, there has not been much formal investigation into shared basis matrices for flat matrix formats until the recent work of
[
6
]
.
(It is noted in
[
6
]
that the construction of HBS matrices in
[
18
]
relies upon a flat format utilizing shared bases, but only as an intermediary step to the HBS format.)
As such,
uniform BLR matrices
, as we will refer to them throughout this work in connotation of their connection to uniform
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-matrices, have not been given a thorough treatment in the existing literature of rank-structured matrices, specifically in the area of randomized rank-structured matrix compression.

Though the BLR format has enjoyed much practical success and performance optimization
[
1
,
11
,
30
,
48
,
50
]
, its utility in many applications has not yet been explored, particularly those in which the matrix entries cannot be directly accessed.
Rather, in these black-box problems, it is assumed that we can only interact with the input matrix through some fast algorithm to quickly evaluate matrix-vector products.
In these applications, the goal is often matrix “reconstruction” in terms of low-rank basis matrices, which enables downstream matrix operations (e.g. inversion or LU factorization) and simplifies operations involving products of rank-structured matrices.
These compressed matrix representations have broad applicability in scientific computing, for instance in deriving rank-structured representations of integral operators
[
44
,
55
]
or accelerating sparse direct solvers
[
41
,
56
,
54
]
.

Randomized algorithms have proven to be very effective in handling the black-box problem environment, particularly the method of
randomized sketching
, in which the row and column spaces of the input matrix are approximated by analyzing how the matrix and its transpose act on tall thin matrices drawn from random matrix distributions
[
26
,
35
,
38
,
43
]
.
Namely, suppose that
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
has some given rank structure where the numerical ranks of admissible blocks are upper bounded by
k\ll N
, but that
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
is only accessible through some fast black-box algorithm.
In order words, given tall thin
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times r}
,
r=\mathcal{O}(k)
, we can quickly evaluate
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
.
Our goal is then to reconstruct
\bm{\mathsf{A}}

as efficiently as possible, using only the information in the set
\{\bm{\mathsf{Y}},\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{Z}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\}
, in a particular rank-structured matrix representation.

Recently in
[
35
]
, Levitt and Martinsson introduced the first fully black-box linear-complexity randomized algorithm to compress (weakly admissible) HBS matrices, inspired by the “peeling algorithm” of
[
38
]
as well as its improvement in
[
36
]
.
The “block nullification” algorithm of
[
35
]
requires only
\mathcal{O}(k)
matrix-vector products (with modest pre-factor) and
\mathcal{O}(k^{2}N)
floating point operations to compress an
N\times N
HBS matrix.
However, to compress flat rank structure formats, linear sampling complexity is not attainable, and in the setting of strong admissibility, the larger pre-factor in computing basis matrices with block nullification presents a significant drawback.

In this manuscript, we present a modification of the algorithm of
[
35
]
for strongly admissible uniform BLR matrices.
We then introduce a new randomized compression algorithm for uniform BLR matrices based on a method we refer to as
tagging
, in which null spaces of small submatrices are computed to exclude contributions from inadmissible blocks in the random sketches
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}
.
As in the block nullification method, the strategic exclusion of inadmissible blocks permits the same sample matrices
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}
to be used to compress every admissible block-row and block-column of the input matrix, which can be straightforwardly parallelized for optimized performance.
Additionally, our tagging method has a smaller asymptotic prefactor than block nullification, and its sampling complexity for basis matrix computation is independent of the problem size even in flat formats, improving on the performance of block nullification without significantly impacting the accuracy of the approximation.

Contributions

We propose two new randomized compression schemes for strongly admissible flat rank-structured matrices with shared bases, termed uniform BLR.
The first scheme is based on our extension of the block nullification method in
[
35
]
to (hierarchical or flat) rank-structured matrices with shared bases under a strong admissibility criterion.
The second randomized compression scheme is our main contribution, which introduces the novel method of tagging for basis matrix computation in the compression of uniform BLR matrices.
We draw a theoretical connection between the tagging matrices in our method and Plücker coordinates in projective space that would guarantee optimal performance, before presenting a practical alternative to compute tagging matrices that is more computationally efficient and works well in practice.
We provide detailed analysis of the asymptotic complexities of both schemes, and we empirically compare their performances in compressing strongly admissible uniform BLR matrices that arise in discretizations of boundary integral equations and sparse direct solvers to demonstrate the superior computational efficiency of our method.

Outline

This manuscript is structured as follows. Section
2
covers the necessary linear algebra preliminaries for our work, as well as background on the uniform BLR matrix format used to illustrate our methods.
Section
3
illustrates our modification of the block nullification method of
[
35
]
for basis matrix computations and analyzes the associated asymptotic complexity.
Section
4
describes the new method of tagging for basis matrix computations including detailed complexity analysis, and Section
5
outlines the theoretical connection between tagging and projective varieties, as well as computational strategies to generate good quality random sketches with tagging in practice.
Finally, Section
6
finishes the compression procedure for uniform BLR matrices.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we briefly summarize the necessary background for randomized compression of the rank-structured matrices considered in our work.
We follow the presentation of
[
40
]
for the requisite linear algebra material, and we provide a synopsis of the block low-rank matrix format introduced in
[
6
]
.

2.1
Notation

A vector
\bm{\mathsf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is measured by the Euclidean norm
\|\bm{\mathsf{x}}\|=\left(\sum_{i}|x_{i}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and
a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is equipped with the corresponding operator norm

\|\bm{\mathsf{A}}\|=\sup_{\|\bm{\mathsf{x}}\|=1}\|\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{x}}\|
.
We let
[m]
denote the integers
1,2,\ldots,m
.
We adopt the notation of Golub and Van Loan
[
20
]
to reference submatrices;
namely, if
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
is an
m\times n
matrix,
and
I=[i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}]\subset[m]

and
J=[j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{l}]\subset[n]
are (row and column, resp.) index sets,
then
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I,J)
denotes the
k\times l
matrix

\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I,J)=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{1},j_{1})&\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{1},j_{2})&\dots&\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{1},j_{l})\\
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{2},j_{1})&\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{2},j_{2})&\dots&\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{2},j_{l})\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{k},j_{1})&\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{k},j_{2})&\dots&\bm{\mathsf{A}}(i_{k},j_{l})\\
\end{bmatrix}.

The abbreviation
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I,:)
is used to designate the submatrix

\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I,[n])
,
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(:,J)
is defined analogously.
The (Hermitian) transpose of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
is given by
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}
,
and a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
is said to be
orthonormal

if its columns are orthonormal,
\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{U}}=\bm{\mathsf{I}}
.

2.2
The QR factorization

Every
m\times n
matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
has a (full) QR factorization of the form

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\bm{\mathsf{B}}&=&\bm{\mathsf{Q}}&\bm{\mathsf{R}},\\
m\times n&&m\times m&m\times n\end{array}

(1)

where

\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
is orthonormal
and

\bm{\mathsf{R}}
is upper-triangular.
If
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
has rank
k

and its first
k
columns are linearly independent,
then it has a rank-
k
partial QR factorization
given by

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\bm{\mathsf{B}}_{k}&=&\bm{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}&\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{k},\\
m\times n&&m\times k&k\times n\end{array}

where
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}_{k}
is orthonormal
and
\bm{\mathsf{R}}_{k}
is upper-triangular.

2.3
Functions for orthonormal bases

For a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
of rank at most
k
, we denote a function that returns a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}

with
k
orthonormal columns spanning the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
by

\bm{\mathsf{Q}}=\texttt{col}(\bm{\mathsf{B}},k),

which can be implemented by truncating a full QR factorization to obtain a rank-
k
partial QR factorization.

For a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
with a null space of at least dimension
k
, we denote a function that returns a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}

with
k
orthonormal columns in the null space of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
by

\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\texttt{null}(\bm{\mathsf{B}},k),

which can be implemented by selecting the last
k
columns of the factor
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
in the full QR factorization of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}^{*}
.

Remark 2.1

If the first
k
columns of a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}

have smaller rank than
\rank(\bm{\mathsf{B}})
,
then the first
k
columns of
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}

produced by an unpivoted QR factorization algorithm
might not span the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
.
There is a similar concern for the linear dependence of rows
when computing a basis for the null space.
Because we only apply
col
and
null

to matrices
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
that are random matrices or products involving random matrices,
any subset of
k
rows or columns will have the same rank as
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
as long as
\rank(\bm{\mathsf{B}})\leq k
; thus, we can
can safely rely on unpivoted QR factorizations in the functions
col
and
null
.

2.4

Randomized range-finding

Let
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
is an
m\times n
matrix
that can be accurately approximated by a rank-
k
matrix, and suppose we seek a matrix whose columns form an approximate orthornomal basis (ON-basis) for the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
.
Often referred to as range-finding, we want to determine an orthonormal matrix
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
such that

\|\bm{\mathsf{B}}-\bm{\mathsf{Q}}\bm{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{B}}\|

is small.
We can accomplish this task efficiently through randomized sketching, where the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
is approximated by analyzing how
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
acts on matrices drawn from random matrix distributions
[
33
,
31
,
26
,
51
]
.
In general, the randomized range-finding algorithm proceeds as follows:

1.

Choose a small integer
p
representing
how much “oversampling” is done (
p=10
is often sufficient).

2.

Draw an
n\times(k+p)
random matrix
\bm{\mathsf{G}}
(e.g. Gaussian
[
33
,
26
]
, randomized Fourier transform
[
10
,
37
]
).

3.

Form the
m\times(k+p)
random sketch
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{B}}\bm{\mathsf{G}}
.

4.

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}=\texttt{col}(\bm{\mathsf{Y}},k)
.

We note that each column of
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
is a random linear combination of the columns of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
, and the probability of obtaining an accurate column space approximation of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
with the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
approaches 1 rapidly as
p
increases;
notably, this probability depends only on
p

(not on
m
or
n
, or any other properties of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
);
cf.
[
26
]
and
[
42
, Section 11]
.

2.5

Block Low-Rank (BLR) Matrices

\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,8}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{1,1}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{2,2}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,3}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{4,4}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{5,5}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{6,6}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{7,7}
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{8,8}

Figure 1
:
Tessellation of a strongly admissible BLR matrix with
b=8
block-rows and block-columns.
Low-rank blocks are shown in gray. The blocks that are not treated as low-rank are shown in red.

This manuscript focuses on the randomized compression of
N\times N
matrices
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
that admit a block low-rank (BLR) format.
BLR matrices are tessellated into
b
row and
b
column blocks according to a “flat” (vs. hierarchical) rank structure.
An example of a BLR matrix is illustrated in Figure
1
.

Typically, a strong admissibility condition is assumed for BLR matrices, as opposed to weak admissibility where every off-diagonal block is treated as low-rank, since the asymptotic complexity is the same as it is with a weak admissibility condition for flat formats
[
6
]
.
Under a strong admissibility condition, the matrix blocks that correspond to the
\leq 3^{d}
neighbors of a given box in a
d
-dimensional geometry are also treated as full rank.

2.5.1

Uniform BLR matrices

In hierarchically rank-structured formats where all levels of the index tree are considered in compression, the computation of orthonormal matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
, whose columns form approximate bases of the column and row spaces of the input matrix, respectively, has been accelerated by nested or shared basis assumptions.

To illustrate, consider an
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-matrix
\bm{\mathsf{A}}

[
25
,
38
]
, a hierarchical matrix which is characterized by each admissible block
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell,m}:=\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I_{\ell},I_{m})
having its own basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell,m}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell,m}
in a rank-
k
representation:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell,m}}_{m\times m}=\underbrace{\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell,m}}_{m\times k}\underbrace{\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\ell,m}}_{k\times k}\underbrace{\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell,m}}_{k\times m}.

(2)

The matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell,m}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell,m}
in (
2
) can be computed via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell,m}&=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell,m},k\right),\\
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{\ell,m}&=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell,m}^{*},k\right),\end{split}

(3)

resulting in a total of
\mathcal{O}(b^{2})
basis matrix computations for
\mathcal{O}(b^{2})
admissible blocks.

In contrast, uniform
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-matrices
[
36
,
38
]

have the property that low-rank blocks in the same block-row or block-column share basis matrices (cf. Figure
2
), resulting in a total of
\mathcal{O}(b)
basis matrix computations for
\mathcal{O}(b^{2})
admissible blocks.
However, compressing the flat analog of uniform
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-matrices has not been thoroughly investigated since the recent introduction of this format by Ashcraft et al. in
[
6
]
.
To this end, throughout this work we refer to BLR matrices with shared bases as uniform BLR matrices.

2.5.2

Obtaining compressed representations of uniform BLR matrices

More formally, a uniform BLR matrix
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
is a flatly tessellated rank-structured matrix for which low-rank blocks within the same block-row or block-column share the same bases of their row or column spaces.
Figure
2
illustrates this property which characterizes uniform BLR matrix, using the BLR matrix from Figure
1
.

To compress (strongly admissible) uniform BLR matrices, we compute basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{m}
for low-rank block
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell,m}
such that

\displaystyle\underbrace{\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell,m}}_{m\times m}=\underbrace{\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell}}_{m\times k}\underbrace{\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{\ell,m}}_{k\times k}\underbrace{\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*}_{m}}_{k\times m},

(4)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{\ell}&=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I_{\ell},I_{i\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}),k\right),\\
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{m}&=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(I_{i\in\mathcal{F}_{m}},I_{m}),k\right).\end{split}

(5)

Here, if
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
denotes the set of neighbors of box
\ell
(so
|\mathcal{N}_{\ell}|\leq 3^{d}
), let
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}=[b]\setminus\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
denote the set complement of
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
in
[b]
, called the far-field of box
\ell
,
and similarly for
\mathcal{F}_{m}
.
As before, the shared bases assumption used in (
5
) reduces the number of basis matrices required for compression, as compared to (
3
), from
\mathcal{O}(b^{2})
to
\mathcal{O}(b)
for a
b\times b
flat tessellation.

When (
4
) holds, we obtain a block factorization of an
N\times N
uniform BLR matrix
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
with
b
blocks in each block-row and block-column, each block of size
m\times m
(letting
N=bm
for notational convenience):

\displaystyle\underset{bm\times bm}{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\underset{bm\times bk}{\bm{\mathsf{U}}}\ \underset{bk\times bk}{\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}}\ \underset{bk\times bm}{\bm{\mathsf{V}}}^{*}+\underset{bm\times bm}{\bm{\mathsf{B}}},

(6)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\mathsf{U}}&=\mbox{diag}(\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{b}),\\
\bm{\mathsf{V}}&=\mbox{diag}(\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{b}),\end{split}

(7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{V}},

(8)

and
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
is a block-sparse matrix defined (for strongly admissible
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
) as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{B}}_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{i,j}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{i,j}\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{j}^{*},&i\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\\
\mathbf{0},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(9)

The matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
given by (
9
) represents a discrepancy term, corresponding to the “remainder” of the inadmissible blocks of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
after their components spanned by the basis matrices have been peeled off
[
38
,
35
]
.

In general, compression of a uniform BLR matrix can be accomplished through the following steps
1
1

1

Steps
(II)
and
(III)
are interchangeable depending on the chosen compression algorithm; see Section
6
for details.
:

(I)

Compute basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
.

(II)

Compute matrix
\tilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}
.

(III)

Compute discrepancy matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*}
.

The primary focus of this work is step
(I)
: we develop and compare randomized algorithms for computing basis matrices of uniform BLR matrices under strong admissibility conditions.
However, for completeness, we outline in Section
6
how the random sketches used for step
(I)
can be recycled for steps
(II)
and
(III)
.
First, we describe the two algorithms that we use to compute basis matrices: the existing method of block nullification in Section
3
and our new method of tagging in Section
4
.

For example, to compute
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,5}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{3,5}\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{5}^{*}
(white):

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\texttt{col}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{blue!30!white}{$\ma_{3,1}$}}\ ,\ \mbox{\framebox{$\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,5}$}}\ ,\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{blue!30!white}{$\ma_{3,6}$}}\ ,\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{blue!30!white}{$\ma_{3,7}$}}\ ,\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{blue!30!white}{$\ma_{3,8}$}}{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}j}\end{bmatrix},k\right)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{5}

\displaystyle=\texttt{col}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{yellow!30!white}{$\ma_{1,5}$}}\ ;\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{yellow!30!white}{$\ma_{2,5}$}}\ ;\ \mbox{\framebox{$\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,5}$}}\ ;\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{yellow!30!white}{$\ma_{7,5}$}}\ ;\ \mbox{\fcolorbox{black}{yellow!30!white}{$\ma_{8,5}$}}{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}j}\end{bmatrix}^{*},k\right)

\displaystyle\vskip 14.22636pt\Rightarrow\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}_{3,5}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3,5}\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{5}.

Figure 2
:
Computing basis matrices for a strongly-admissible uniform BLR matrix using
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
from Figure
1
.

3

Block Nullification in Uniform BLR Matrix Compression

In this section, we present a modification of the previous work of
[
35
]
to develop a linear randomized compression algorithm for hierarchically block-separable (HBS) rank-structured matrices.
This compression algorithm utilizes “block nullification” to form random sketches of admissible matrix blocks; these sketches are then used to compute basis matrices in an HBS representation according to the randomized rangefinding procedure of Section
2.4
.
The algorithm is also fully black-box, so that steps
(I)
-
(III)
above can be accomplished without access to individual matrix entries.
Rather, it assumes access to fast black-box matrix multiplication, so that sample matrices
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
can be formed efficiently given tall thin random test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times s}
for
s=\mathcal{O}(k)
for block-rank
k
.

The goal is then to “reconstruct”
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
via steps
(I)
-
(III)
using only the matrices
\bm{\mathsf{Y}},\bm{\mathsf{Z}},\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
, and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
(computed a priori) by the randomized rangefinder procedure in Section
2.4
.
However, each row of
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
, for instance, is a random linear combination of all columns of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
within a given block-row, including the columns belonging to inadmissible blocks.
Block nullification yields “clean” random sketches from
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}
by excluding contributions from inadmissible blocks, without repeatedly applying
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
or
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}
to tailored random test matrices that individually sample admissible blocks in each block-row or block-column.

While block nullification is suitable for flat or hierarchical rank-structured formats, its performance has only been investigated for hierarchically block-separable (HBS) matrices.
As such, we first modify the block nullification procedure in this section to accommodate uniform BLR matrices under a strong admissibility condition.
We then discuss its asymptotic complexity to emphasize that block nullification yields a larger pre-factor than our proposed technique in the next section.

3.1

Block nullification for strongly-admissible uniform BLR matrices

We begin with an illustrative example of the block nullification technique applied to the strongly-admissible uniform BLR matrix
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
from Figure
1
, flatly tessellated into
b
block-rows and block-columns each of size
m\times m
, with admissible blocks of rank
k
.
Let
r=k+p
for a small oversampling parameter (e.g.
p=10
), and let
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times s}
be Gaussian test matrices with
s\geq\max(r+3m,3r)
.

Suppose that we want to compute the basis matrix
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
for the block-row of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
as in Figure
2
, now using the sketch
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
as in the randomized rangefinder procedure.
Consider a random sketch of the form

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccc}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}&=&\bm{\mathsf{A}}&\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}\\

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

where
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}=\bm{\mathsf{Y}}(I_{3},:)
, and the test matrix blocks
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{i}=\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}(I_{i},:)
for
i=1,\ldots,8
are color-coded according to their respective block-factors in the block-row
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I_{3},:)
.
As before, the blue blocks of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
are the admissible blocks in
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3}
whose columns will be approximately spanned by
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}
computed with
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}
, whereas the red blocks of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
are inadmissible; our goal is to exclude their contributions from the randomized sample of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3}
held in
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}
.

Since
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(3)}:=\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}([I_{2},I_{3},I_{4}],:)=\left[\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}|\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}|\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}\right]^{*}
is of size
3m\times s
, it has a nullspace of dimension at least
s-3m\geq r
.
We then compute a set of
r
orthonormal vectors in its null space:

\displaystyle\underset{s\times r}{\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}}=\texttt{null}(\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(3)},r):=\texttt{null}(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}\\
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}\\
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}\end{bmatrix},r)\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}\\
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}\\
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}\end{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}=\underset{3m\times r}{\bm{0}}.

(10)

Thus, we can obtain the desired sample of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{3}
inexpensively from
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
via
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}
, shown below in blue:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccc}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}&=&\bm{\mathsf{A}}&\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}\\

noting that the white blocks of
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}
are filled with zeros.
We also note that the blue blocks of
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}
contain standard Gaussian entries
because (1) the distribution of Gaussian matrices is invariant under unitary transformations and (2) the matrix
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)}
is computed independently of the blue blocks of
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
.
The desired basis matrix can then be computed via

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}=\texttt{col}(\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(3)},k)

with the usual probabilistic guarantees (cf. Section
2.4
and
[
26
,
42
]
).

In general, the method of block nullification computes basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}
according to Algorithm
1
to accomplish step
(I)
of the randomized compression of uniform BLR matrices as in (
6
).
Quickly summarizing, we first draw independent Gaussian matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
to form random sketches
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}
(lines 1-3).
For any block
i=1,\ldots,b
, we define
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(i)}
as the rows of
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
indexed by
\{I_{j}\}_{j\in N_{i}}
, or all
I_{j}
such that
j
is a neighbor of block
i
.
We then compute
r
orthonormal vectors in the null space of
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(i)}
, which comprise the columns of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(i)}
.
The matrix
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i}=\bm{\mathsf{Y}}(I_{i},:)
is right-multiplied by
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(i)}
to compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}
, whose
k
columns form an approximate basis for the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I_{i},:)
excluding inadmissible blocks (lines 5-6).
Analogously, for each block
i
, we compute the basis matrix
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}
whose column space approximates the column space of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(I_{i},:)
excluding inadmissible blocks (lines 7-8).
We discuss the asymptotic complexity of Algorithm
1
in the next section.

Algorithm 1

Block Nullification for Basis Construction

1:

Fast matrix-vector multiplication with uniform BLR
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
,
b\times b
flat matrix tessellation, maximum block-size
m
,
d
-dimensional geometry

2:

\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times bk}
in uniform BLR representation of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
as in (
6
)

3:

Set
r=k+p
and
s\geq\max(r+3^{d}m,3^{d}r)

4:

Draw independent Gaussian matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times s}

5:

Sketch
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}

6:

for
blocks
i=1,\ldots,b

do

7:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(i)}=\texttt{null}\left(\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(i)},\ r\right)

8:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(i)},\ k\right)

9:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}^{(i)}=\texttt{null}\left(\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}^{(i)},\ r\right)

10:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{Z}}_{i}\bm{\mathsf{Q}}^{(i)},\ k\right)

11:

end

for

12:

Set
\bm{\mathsf{U}}=\textrm{diag}\left(\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{b}\right)
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}=\textrm{diag}\left(\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{b}\right)

3.2

Asymptotic complexity of block nullification

We analyze the asymptotic complexity of Algorithm
1
by following its steps and quantifying the computational costs. Let
r=k+p
, where
k
is the block rank and
p
is the oversampling parameter, and let
s=3^{d}m+r
, with
m
denoting the block size. For the purpose of generality, we assume that
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
is not necessarily self-adjoint. There are savings of a factor of 2 when the matrix is self-adjoint.

•

Gaussian matrix generation (lines 2-3). Generating the random test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
requires sampling
2Ns
values from the standard Gaussian distribution. The cost of this step is
2Ns\times T_{\textup{rand}}
, where
T_{\textup{rand}}
represents the time to sample one value.

•

Matrix-vector products (line 3). Forming the sketches
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
involves
s
matrix-vector multiplications for both
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}
.
This contributes
2s\times T_{\textup{mult}}
to the overall complexity, where
T_{\textup{mult}}
is the cost of applying
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
or
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}
to a vector.

•

Null-space basis Computation (lines 5, 7):
For each block
i=1,\ldots,b
, we compute
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(i)}=\texttt{null}(\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(i)},r)
and
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}^{(i)}=\texttt{null}(\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}^{(i)},r)
. These computations involve matrices of size at most
3^{d}m\times s
. Using Householder QR
[
28
, Table C.2]
for
null
, the cost for one such computation is approximately:

\mathcal{O}\left(3^{3d}m^{3}\right).

Since there are
b
blocks, this step adds:

\mathcal{O}\left(b\times 3^{3d}m^{3}\right)\times T_{\textup{flop}},

where
T_{\textup{flop}}
is the cost of one floating-point arithmetic operation.

•

Column Basis Extraction (Lines 6, 8):
Computing
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}=\texttt{col}(\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{i}\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{(i)},k)
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}=\texttt{col}(\bm{\mathsf{Z}}_{i}\bm{\mathsf{Q}}^{(i)},k)
involves matrix multiplications of dimensions
3^{d}m\times r
with
r\times k
for each block.
The cost of these multiplications across all blocks is:

\mathcal{O}\left(b\times[3^{d}mr^{2}]\right)\times T_{\textup{flop}}.

•

Reconstruction:
Once the basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
are computed, determining the matrix
\bm{\mathsf{D}}
for the full matrix reconstruction as in (
6
) involves additional matrix-vector products. The cost of this step is exactly:

(3^{d}m+kb)\times T_{\textup{mult}}.

Combining the contributions from all steps and using that
N=mb
, we express this as:

2N(3^{d}m+r)\times T_{\textup{rand}}+\mathcal{O}\left(N\times 3^{3d}m^{2}\right)\times T_{\textup{flop}}+(3^{(d+1)}m+2r+kN/m)\times T_{\textup{mult}}.

4

Tagging in Uniform BLR Matrix Compression

We now describe a new black-box randomized method to compress strongly admissible uniform BLR matrices which we call tagging, the main contribution of our manuscript.
As in Section
3
, we begin with an illustrative example to introduce the concept before generalizing to tagging for
d
-dimensional problem geometries and summarizing its asymptotic complexity.

4.1

Tagging for strongly-admissible uniform BLR matrices

Let
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
be an
N\times N
strongly admissible uniform BLR matrix as in Figure
1
, tessellated into
b
blocks of size
m\times m
with uniform block-rank
k
, allowing for a small amount of oversampling given by
p
, and let
r=k+p
.
Our aim once again is to construct random test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times s}
with
s=\mathcal{O}(k)
so that the sketches
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
taken a priori can be used to compress the admissible blocks in every block-row and block-column.

To illustrate, suppose as in Section
3.1
that we want to compute the basis matrix
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
from Figure
2
.
We first introduce the
8\times 4
tagging matrix

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{bmatrix}t_{1,1}&t_{1,2}&t_{1,3}&t_{1,4}\\
t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
t_{8,1}&t_{8,2}&t_{8,3}&t_{8,4}\\
\end{bmatrix},

(11)

where the entries
t_{i,j}
will be made explicit in Section
5
; for now, we treat them as i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries.
We note that the number of rows of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
equals the number of tessellated matrix blocks
b
.
The number of columns of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
is one more than the maximal number of neighbors within a given block-row or block-column, which is
4
for the strongly admissible 1-D matrix in Figure
1
.
In general, for a
d
-dimensional problem geometry, the number of columns of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
is
3^{d}+1
.

We next define the extended random test matrix
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 4r}
in terms of random test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times r}
for
j=1,\ldots,4
, given by

\displaystyle\underset{N\times 4r}{\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}}:=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}}&\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}&\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}&\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}t_{1,1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}&t_{1,2}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}&t_{1,3}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}&t_{1,4}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}\\
t_{2,1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{2}&t_{2,2}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{2}&t_{2,3}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{2}&t_{2,4}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{2}\\
t_{3,1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{3}&t_{3,2}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{3}&t_{3,3}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{3}&t_{3,4}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{3}\\
t_{4,1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{4}&t_{4,2}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{4}&t_{4,3}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{4}&t_{4,4}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{4}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
t_{8,1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{8}&t_{8,2}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{8}&t_{8,3}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{8}&t_{8,4}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{8}\end{bmatrix},

(12)

where each
\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times r}
,
i=1,\ldots,8
, is a Gaussian random matrix, weighted by entry
t_{i,j}
of the tagging matrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
to form
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,4
.
Note that we again assume
N=bm
for notational convenience.
We form the sketch matrix
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 4r}
, partitioned into
N\times r
block-columns commensurately with (
12
) so that

\displaystyle\underset{N\times 4r}{\bm{\mathsf{Y}}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}&\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}&\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}&\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}&\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}&\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}&\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}.

To compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
as in Figure
2
, we exclude contributions from inadmissible blocks in the third block-row by computing a (nonzero) vector
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(3)}=\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}^{(3)}&z_{2}^{(3)}&z_{3}^{(3)}&z_{4}^{(3)}\end{bmatrix}^{*}

so that

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(3)}=\texttt{null}\left(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(3)}\right):=\texttt{null}\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,2}&t_{3,3}&t_{3,4}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,2}&t_{4,3}&t_{4,4}\end{bmatrix}\right).

(13)

Note that this submatrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(3)}
of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
comprises the rows that correspond to the neighbor list
\mathcal{N}_{3}=[2:4]
of block 3.
Now consider the weighted sum

\displaystyle z_{1}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}+z_{2}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}+z_{3}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}+z_{4}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}=\begin{bmatrix}(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{1,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{1,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{1,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{1,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{2,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{2,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{2,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{2,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{2}\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{3,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{3,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{3,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{3,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{3}\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{4,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{4,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{4,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{4,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{4}\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{5,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{5,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{5,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{5,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{5}\\
\vdots\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{8,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{8,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{8,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{8,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{8}\end{bmatrix}.

By construction, it simplifies to

\displaystyle z_{1}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}+z_{2}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}+z_{3}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}+z_{4}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4}=\begin{bmatrix}(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{1,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{1,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{1,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{1,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}\\
\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{5,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{5,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{5,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{5,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{5}\\
\vdots\\
(z_{1}^{(3)}t_{8,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{8,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{8,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{8,4})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{8}\end{bmatrix},

(14)

so that the rows of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(z_{1}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}+z_{2}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}+z_{3}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3}+z_{4}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{4})=z_{1}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}+z_{2}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}+z_{3}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}+z_{4}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{4}
corresponding to
I_{3}
will contain the desired sample of the admissible blocks for computing
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}
, with the contributions from inadmissible blocks now zeroed out.
We can then compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{3}=\texttt{col}\left((z_{1}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}+z_{2}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}+z_{3}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3}+z_{4}^{(3)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{4})(I_{3},:),k\right)
as in the randomized rangefinding procedure.

In general, the tagging method computes basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
according to Algorithm
2
, which we quickly summarize.
We begin by drawing the entries of the tagging matrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times(3^{d}+1)}
e.g. from a standard Gaussian distribution (line 1-2).
We next form the random sketches

\displaystyle\underset{N\times(3^{d}+1)r}{\bm{\mathsf{Y}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{1}&\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{3^{d}+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}&\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{3^{d}+1}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},

(15)

\displaystyle\underset{N\times(3^{d}+1)r}{\bm{\mathsf{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{Z}}_{1}&\bm{\mathsf{Z}}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{\mathsf{Z}}_{3^{d}+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}_{1}&\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}_{2}&\ldots&\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}_{3^{d}+1}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}.

(16)

where each
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,3^{d}+1
, comprises
b
block-rows of independent Gaussian matrices
\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times r}
,
i=1,\ldots,b
, weighted by tagging entry
t_{i,j}
as in (
12
), and similarly for each
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}_{j}
with
\bm{\mathsf{H}}_{i}
(lines 3-8).
To compute the basis matrix
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}
for any block
i
, we focus on the submatrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}
of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
comprising rows indexed by
\mathcal{N}_{i}
, the list of neighbors of block
i
so that
|\mathcal{N}_{i}|\leq 3^{d}
.
We then compute an orthonormal tagging vector
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}=\begin{bmatrix}z^{(i)}_{1}z^{(i)}_{2}\ldots z^{(i)}_{3^{d}+1}\end{bmatrix}^{*}
in the nontrivial null space of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}
(line 10).
Finally, we compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
(lines 11-12) whose columns are orthonormal basis vectors approximating the column spaces of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}(I_{i},:)
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(I_{i},:)
, excluding contributions from the inadmissible blocks in block-row
i
.

Algorithm 2

Tagging for Basis Construction

1:

Fast matrix-vector multiplication with uniform BLR
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
,
b\times b
flat matrix tessellations, maximum block-size
m
,
d
-dimensional geometry

2:

\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times bk}
in uniform BLR representation of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
as in (
6
)

3:

Set
r=k+p
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}=[\ ]
.

4:

Form tagging matrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times(3^{d}+1)}

\triangleright

e.g. Gaussian
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
; see Section
5

5:

for
blocks
i=1,\ldots,b

do

6:

Draw independent Gaussian matrices
\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{H}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times r}

7:

Update
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}&;&\begin{bmatrix}t_{i,1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i}&\ldots&t_{i,3^{d}+1}\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

\triangleright

Append
i^{th}
row to
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}

8:

Update
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}&;&\begin{bmatrix}t_{i,1}\bm{\mathsf{H}}_{i}&\ldots&t_{i,3^{d}+1}\bm{\mathsf{H}}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

\triangleright

Append
i^{th}
row to
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}

9:

end

for

10:

Sketch
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}

11:

for
blocks
i=1,\ldots,b

do

12:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}=\texttt{null}\left(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}\right)

13:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}=\texttt{col}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3^{d}+1}z_{j}^{(i)}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{j}^{(i)},\ k\right)

14:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}=\texttt{col}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3^{d}+1}z_{j}^{(i)}\bm{\mathsf{Z}}_{j}^{(i)},\ k\right)

15:

end

for

16:

\bm{\mathsf{U}}=\textrm{diag}\left(\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{b}\right)
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}=\textrm{diag}\left(\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{b}\right)

4.2

Asymptotic Complexity of Tagging

We derive the asymptotic complexity of Algorithm
2
in terms of the problem size
N
, block size
m
, block-rank
k
, oversampling parameter
p
, and problem geometry dimension
d
. Let
r=k+p
and assume
N=mb
, where
b
is the number of matrix blocks in each block-row or block-column.
The complexity of the algorithm is broken into the following components:

•

Formation of the Tagging Matrix (Lines 3-4):
The tagging matrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times(3^{d}+1)}
requires
(3^{d}+1)N/m
elements to be sampled from a Gaussian distribution. The total time is:

(3^{d}+1)\frac{N}{m}\times T_{\textup{rand}}.

•

Formation of Random Test Matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
(Lines 5–8):
Random test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times(3^{d}+1)r}
involves sampling
\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{H}}_{i}
for each subblock and forming the test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
by scaling each block by the relevant tag.
Sampling contributes
2Nr\cdot T_{\textup{rand}}
, as each Gaussian matrix
\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{i}
and
\bm{\mathsf{H}}_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,b
) is reused.
Forming
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
requires
2(3^{d}+1)Nr
floating-point operations, contributing
2(3^{d}+1)Nr\cdot T_{\textup{flop}}
.
The total complexity for
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
is:

2Nr\times T_{\textup{rand}}+\mathcal{O}(3^{d}Nr)\times T_{\textup{flop}}.

•

Matrix-Vector Products for Sampling (Line 9):
Computing the sample matrices
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
involves
2(3^{d}+1)r
matrix-vector products, with complexity:

2(3^{d}+1)r\times T_{\textup{mult}}.

•

Null Space Computation (Lines 11–12):
For each block, the null space computation involves matrices of size at most
3^{d}\times(3^{d}+1)
. Using Householder reflections, the computational cost is:

\mathcal{O}\left(3^{3d}b\right)\cdot T_{\textup{flop}}.

•

Basis Matrix Construction (Lines 13–15):
Forming the basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
involves multipling the relevant subblocks by the null space vector, then computing an orthogonal basis.
This involves
(3^{d}+1)mr
floating-point operations per block to scale submatrices of
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}
and
\mathcal{O}(rmk+k^{3})
floating-point operations per block for
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}
.
The total complexity is:

\mathcal{O}(3^{d}Nr+Nrk+Nk^{3}/m)\times T_{\textup{flop}}.

•

Reconstruction:
Reconstructing the uniform BLR matrix using
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
requires additional matrix-vector products and contributes

(3^{d}m+kb)\times T_{\textup{mult}}.

Combining the above contributions, the overall complexity is:

\left((3^{d}+1)\frac{N}{m}+2Nr\right)\cdot T_{\textup{rand}}+\mathcal{O}\left(3^{d}Nr+Nrk+\frac{Nk^{3}}{m}+3^{3d}\frac{N}{m}\right)\times T_{\textup{flop}}+(2\cdot 3^{d+1}r+3^{d}m+kb)\times T_{\textup{mult}}.

Comparing to block nullification in Section
3.2
, far fewer samples are needed to construct the basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
. Block nullification requires
2\times 3^{d}m
samples for basis construction in Section
3.2
, whereas tagging only
requires
2\times 3^{d+1}r
samples. The cost of reconstructing the uniform BLR matrix, however,
dominates the asymptotic complexity of
T_{\textup{mult}}
for both methods.
The key advantage of tagging is the substantially reduced cost of post-processing the test and sketch matrices, with linear post-processing cost, as opposed to block-nullification, which scales linearly in
N
and quadratically with the block size.

5

Selecting the Tagging Matrix

In its introduction in Section
4.1
, we treated the tagging matrix as having standard Gaussian entries.
However, standard Gaussian tagging matrix entries do not guarantee standard Gaussian samples of the input matrix.
As such, we seek to address the following questions. Does there exist an optimal tagging matrix that yields Gaussian samples? If so, what is it? If not, how closely can we approximate one?

To answer these questions, we must first be explicit about what constitutes optimality, so we discuss the criteria in Section
5.1
.
We then offer a conjecture on the existence of optimal tagging matrices in Section
5.2
which takes an algebraic-geometric perspective on tagging matrix optimality.
We finish the section by presenting a highly efficient alternative strategy to determine tagging matrices that perform well empirically despite their sub-optimality.

5.1

On the optimality of tagging matrices: Projected tags and aspect ratios

The main issue that we need to address in tagging matrix selection concerns the projected tags, the nonzero non-uniform weights on each Gaussian matrix in (
14
) from Section
4.1
.
Recall that for any block
i=1,\ldots,b
with neighbor list
\mathcal{N}_{i}
, we compute
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}=\begin{bmatrix}z^{(i)}_{1}&z^{(i)}_{2}&\ldots&z^{(i)}_{\ell}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{null}(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)})
, letting
\ell=3^{d}+1
, for sub-matrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}=\bm{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{N}_{i},:)
.
Then consider the linear combination

\displaystyle z_{1}^{(i)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{1}+z_{2}^{(i)}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{2}+\ldots+z^{(i)}_{\ell}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(t_{1,1}z_{1}^{(i)}+t_{1,2}z_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots t_{1,\ell}z_{\ell}^{(i)})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{1}\\
(t_{2,1}z_{1}^{(i)}+t_{2,2}z_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots t_{2,\ell}z_{\ell}^{(i)})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
(t_{b,1}z_{1}^{(i)}+t_{b,2}z_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots t_{b,\ell}z_{\ell}^{(i)})\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{b}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

(17)

Note that the coefficients of
\bm{\mathsf{G}}_{j}
(i.e. projected tags) are 0 by construction for any
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
.
However, the nonzero projected tags, which correspond to the far-field
\mathcal{F}_{i}=[b]\setminus\mathcal{N}_{i}
of block
i
, non-uniformly weight each Gaussian matrix, resulting in non-uniformly weighted randomized samples of blocks within the same block-row or block-column of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
.
Each of the projected tags should ideally be equal in magnitude.

To this end, we define the aspect ratio
\rho^{(i)}
for block-row or block-column
i=1,\ldots,b
as the largest-magnitude to the smallest-magnitude nonzero projected tag:

\displaystyle\rho^{(i)}=\frac{\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}|t_{j,1}z_{1}^{(i)}+t_{j,2}z_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots+t_{j,\ell}z^{(i)}_{\ell}|}{\min\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}|t_{j,1}z_{1}^{(i)}+t_{j,2}z_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots+t_{j,\ell}z^{(i)}_{\ell}|}\geq 1.

(18)

Our goal is then to determine an optimal tagging matrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
for which the projected tags minimize
\rho^{(i)}
for each
i=1,\ldots,b
.

In the following subsections, we examine the tagging matrix optimality problem through two different lenses.
The first relies on ideas from algebraic geometry to determine an optimal tagging matrix.
The second offers a computational short-cut via null space vectors that minimize aspect ratios through a fast numerical optimization scheme.

5.2

On the existence of optimal tagging matrices: Plücker coordinates

To express our conjecture on optimal tagging matrices, we draw a connection between tagging matrices and projective varieties in algebraic geometry through Plücker coordinates.
We return to our familiar example of a uniform BLR matrix from Figure
1
for an intuitive introduction to the Plücker embedding that gives rise to Plücker coordinates.
We then hypothesize that optimal tagging matrices may be found through a hybrid numeric-symbolic approach based on Plücker coordinates, which is currently out of computational reach.

5.2.1

An illustrative example

Let
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be the uniform BLR matrix from Figure
1
.
Note that for blocks
i=2,\ldots,7
, we can apply Cramer’s Rule to determine
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}=[z_{1}^{(i)},z_{2}^{(i)},z_{3}^{(i)},z_{4}^{(i)}]^{*}
from
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}
, e.g. the
i^{th}
coordinate
z_{i}^{(3)}
of
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(3)}
can be computed as the determinant of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(3)}
without the
i^{th}
column:

\displaystyle\begin{split}z_{1}^{(3)}&=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}\\
t_{3,2}&t_{3,3}&t_{3,4}\\
t_{4,2}&t_{4,3}&t_{4,4}\end{bmatrix}\right),\qquad z_{2}^{(3)}=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,1}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,3}&t_{3,4}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,3}&t_{4,4}\end{bmatrix}\right),\\
z_{3}^{(3)}&=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,4}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,2}&t_{3,4}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,2}&t_{4,4}\end{bmatrix}\right),\qquad z_{4}^{(3)}=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,2}&t_{3,3}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,2}&t_{4,3}\end{bmatrix}\right).\end{split}

(19)

Then notice that the vector of projected tags
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}
contains all
4\times 4
subdeterminants of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
that can be formed from the three rows of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}
plus one remaining row of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
, e.g. for
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(3)}
using (
19
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(3)}&=\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}^{(3)}t_{1,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{1,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{1,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{1,4}\\
\vdots\\
z_{1}^{(3)}t_{8,1}+z_{2}^{(3)}t_{8,2}+z_{3}^{(3)}t_{8,3}+z_{4}^{(3)}t_{8,4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{1,1}&t_{1,2}&t_{1,3}&t_{1,4}\\
t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,2}&t_{3,3}&t_{3,4}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,2}&t_{4,3}&t_{4,4}\end{bmatrix}\right)\\
\vdots\\
\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,2}&t_{3,3}&t_{3,4}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,2}&t_{4,3}&t_{4,4}\\
t_{8,1}&t_{8,2}&t_{8,3}&t_{8,4}\\
\end{bmatrix}\right)\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

(20)

When
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
has full rank, the
4\times 4
determinants in each coordinate of the projected tags
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}
for
i=2,\ldots,7
form a subset of the Plücker relations
2
2

2

We treat the “extremal” blocks
i=1
and
i=8
(in general, blocks with fewer than the maximal number of neighbors
3^{d}
) at the end of the section.
, the set of all possible
4\times 4
determinants from the the rows of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
.
Now, let
\mathcal{L}
be a 4-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
with
\textrm{Col}(\bm{\mathsf{T}})=\mathcal{L}
.
We define the Plücker embedding as the map from
\mathcal{L}
to the point in real projective space whose coordinates are all
4\times 4
determinants of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{8\times 4}
.
In algebraic-geometric terms, the Plücker embedding maps the Grassmannian manifold
\textrm{Gr}(4,8)
comprising all
4
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, to Plücker coordinates in
\mathbb{P}^{\binom{8}{4}-1}
, as stated formally below:

Definition 5.1

The
Plücker embedding
is the map
\textup{Gr}(k,n)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{\binom{n}{k}-1}
that identifies
\mathcal{V}\in\textup{Gr}(k,n)
with a unique point in real projective space, whose coordinates are given by all
k\times k
determinants of a matrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
satisfying
\mathcal{V}=\textup{Col}(\bm{\mathsf{B}})
, called
Plücker coordinates
.

Definition 5.2

Let
\mathcal{V}\in\textup{Gr}(k,n)
and suppose
\bm{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
satisfies
\mathcal{V}=\textup{Col}(\bm{\mathsf{B}})
.
For any ordered sequence of
k
row indices
1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n
of
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
, let
B_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
denote the determinant of the
k\times k
submatrix
\bm{\mathsf{B}}([i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}],:)
, so that set of all Plücker coordinates may be denoted
\{B_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\}
.
Then for any two ordered sequences of row indices

\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k-1}\leq n,\qquad 1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{k+1}\leq n,

the
Plücker relations
are the following homogeneous quadratic equations that must hold for all Plücker coordinates
\{B_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{\ell}B_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},j_{\ell}}B_{j_{1},\ldots,\widehat{j}_{\ell},\ldots,j_{k+1}}=0,

(21)

where
j_{1},\ldots,\widehat{j}_{\ell},\ldots,j_{k+1}
is the sequence
j_{1},\ldots,j_{k+1}
with the term
j_{\ell}
omitted.

We can now pose the optimality of tagging matrices in algebraic-geometric terms.

5.2.2

On the existence of optimal tagging matrices

Let
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times 3^{d}+1}
be a matrix of indeterminates
t_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq b
and
1\leq j\leq 3^{d}+1
.
For any ordered sequence of
3^{d}+1
row indices
1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{3^{d}+1}\leq b
of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
, let
T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{3^{d}+1}}
denote the determinant of the
(3^{d}+1)\times(3^{d}+1)
submatrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}([i_{1},\ldots,i_{3^{d}+1}],:)
.
For example, we would let
T_{2,3,4,8}
denote the final
4\times 4
determinant in (
20
) of the submatrix comprising
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(3)}
and the last row of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
.
Note that each determinant
T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{3^{d}+1}}
is a degree-
(3^{d}+1)
polynomial in the indeterminates
t_{i,j}
.
From the previous section, each of these determinants is a Plücker coordinate that must satisfy the Plücker relations, which are quadratic polynomials in the indeterminates
T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{3^{d}+1}}
for all possible ordered sequences
1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{3^{d}+1}\leq b
.

To determine tagging matrix entries
t_{i,j}
that minimize
\rho^{(i)}
for each block
i
, we propose the following approach.
It is well-known that the set of Plücker relations is not algebraically independent, cf.
[
46
, Chapter 14.2]
and
[
27
, Appendix C.7]
.
Thus, the first step is to determine an algebraically independent generating set of Plücker relations for
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
.
One method is the computation of a Gröbner basis using Buchberger’s algorithm
3
3

3

The computation of a Gröbner basis for the quadratic polynomials under consideration is highly nontrivial for problems of this size due to, e.g., intermediate swell in Buchberger’s algorithm; see
[
14
]
.
for the ideal of the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[\{t_{i,j}\}_{i\in[b],j\in[3^{d}+1]}]
generated by all Plücker relations, cf.
[
47
]
.

Remark 5.1

Another avenue of investigation that bears future consideration involves the so-called “clusters” formed by independent Plücker coordinates
[
49
]
.
One such cluster is comprised of rectangular Plücker coordinates
[
34
]
, which correspond to the rectangular partitions of a
k\times(n-k)
unit rectangle and form a generating set for the coordinate ring.
Rectangular Plücker coordinates relate to quantum Schubert calculus on the flag variety
[
7
,
15
,
19
]
and have an associated Laurent polynomial with certain properties
[
39
]
that may offer another path to an optimal tagging matrix.

Now, let
\mathcal{R}
denote a set of algebraically independent Plücker relations that generate the projective variety defined by all Plücker relations.
Recall that for the purposes of tagging, we are only interested in the nonzero projected tags in (
17
), which correspond to the far-field indices of each block.
Let
n
be the total number of nonzero projected tags, with
n_{i}
nonzero projected tags for block
i
, so that
\sum_{i=1}^{b}n_{i}=n
.
Denote by
\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\}
these
n
nonzero projected tags, or
n
nonzero determinants of
(3^{d}+1)\times(3^{d}+1)
submatrices of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
,

\displaystyle\left\{T_{i^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,i^{(1)}_{3^{d}+1}},\ T_{i^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,i^{(2)}_{3^{d}+1}},\ldots,\ T_{i^{(n)}_{1},\ldots,i^{(n)}_{3^{d}+1}}\right\}

(22)

for distinct ordered sequences
1\leq i^{(\ell)}_{1}<\ldots<i^{(\ell)}_{3^{d}+1}\leq b
for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
.

Ideally, for each block
i
, every nonzero projected tag, or nonzero coordinate of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}
, should be equal (cf. (
18
)), which we can enforce numerically via

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\min_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times 3^{d}+1}}\sum_{i=1}^{b}\sum_{\ell_{i}=1}^{n_{i}}(T_{\ell_{i}}-\overline{T})^{2}\\
&\textup{subject to }\ \mathcal{R}\end{split}

(23)

where
\overline{T}
denotes the mean of the
n_{i}
nonzero projected tags for block
i
.
We say an arg min
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{*}
of (
23
) is numerically optimal if
\max_{i}(\rho^{(i)})>1
.

We now note that the outlined approach only holds for blocks that have a neighbor list of maximal size
3^{d}
.
One workaround is to reassign the necessary number of admissible blocks to be inadmissible in the block-rows or block-columns of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
that have fewer than
3^{d}
inadmissible blocks, though this increases the overall cost of reconstruction.
More detrimental, though, is the cost of the symbolic computation required.
While the optimization problem in (
23
) is straightforward, the computations for
\mathcal{R}
are highly nontrivial even for very small values of
b
, constraints which must hold if the arg min
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{*}
of (
23
) satisfies
\textup{Col}(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{*})=\mathcal{V}
for some
(3^{d}+1)
-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathbb{R}^{b}
.
Moreover, if the problem size or underlying geometry were to change, these computations would need to be done anew for different values of
b
or
d
.
In the next section, we describe a numerical method of minimizing the aspect ratios that performs highly efficiently in practice without sacrificing accuracy.

5.3

A practical method for numerical optimization

Because of the difficulties in practice of using the algebraic-geometric approach of Section
5.2
, we now present an alternative way to minimize the aspect ratios numerically for each block
i=1,\ldots,b
.
This practical approach requires higher-dimensional null spaces of tagging submatrices; as such, we will now consider tagging matrices
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times\ell}
where
\ell>3^{d}+1
so that every null space has dimension strictly greater than 1.

To minimize the aspect ratios of projected tags efficiently, we consider the following optimization problem over the
(\ell-3^{d})
-dimensional null space of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{3^{d}\times\ell}
, rather than over all possible matrix representations
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
of a Grassmannian subspace
\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathcal{V}=\textup{Col}(\bm{\mathsf{T}})
.
We now seek a unit vector
\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}
in the null space of
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}
for each block
i=1,\ldots,8
, which minimizes the ratio of projected tags:

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}=\arg\min_{\bm{\mathsf{x}}\in\textup{Null}(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)})}\frac{\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}|t_{j,1}x_{1}^{(i)}+t_{j,2}x_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots+t_{j,\ell}x^{(i)}_{\ell}|}{\min\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}|t_{j,1}x_{1}^{(i)}+t_{j,2}x_{2}^{(i)}+\ldots+t_{j,\ell}x^{(i)}_{\ell}|}.

(24)

We illustrate the procedure for block-row
i=3
as before, adding one extra column to
\bm{\mathsf{T}}
.
Then the tagging submatrix

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(3)}=\begin{bmatrix}t_{2,1}&t_{2,2}&t_{2,3}&t_{2,4}&t_{2,5}\\
t_{3,1}&t_{3,2}&t_{3,3}&t_{3,4}&t_{3,5}\\
t_{4,1}&t_{4,2}&t_{4,3}&t_{4,4}&t_{4,5}\end{bmatrix}

has a 2-dimensional null space, so let
\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(3)}&\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{(3)}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{null}(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(3)})
be an orthonormal basis.
Any unit vector
\bm{\mathsf{x}}
in the null space may be expressed as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{x}}=\cos(\theta)\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(3)}+\sin(\theta)\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{(3)},\qquad\theta\in[0,2\pi].

Thus, we can efficiently determine an optimal null space vector via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta^{*}&=\arg\min_{\theta\in[0,2\pi]}\frac{\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}\left|\left(\cos(\theta)\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(3)}+\sin(\theta)\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{(3)}\right)^{*}\bm{\mathsf{t}}^{(j})\right|}{\min\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}\left|\left(\cos(\theta)\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(3)}+\sin(\theta)\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{(3)}\right)^{*}\bm{\mathsf{t}}^{(j)}\right|},\end{split}

(25)

where
\bm{\mathsf{t}}^{(j)}=\begin{bmatrix}t_{j,1}&t_{j,2}&\ldots&t_{j,\ell}\end{bmatrix}^{*}
for a more concise representation of the objective function, so that the optimal null space vector is

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\cos(\theta^{*})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(3)}+\sin(\theta^{*})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{(3)}.

For arbitrary
i=1,\ldots,b
with
\bm{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times\ell}
and
\ell>3^{d}+1
, the null space of submatrix
\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)}
has dimension at least
s=\ell-3^{d}>1
.
We compute

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(i)}&\ldots&\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{s}^{(i)}\end{bmatrix}=\texttt{null}\left(\bm{\mathsf{T}}^{(i)},s\right),

and write any normalized vector in the null space as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{x}}=\alpha_{1}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(i)}+\ldots+\alpha_{s}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{s}^{(i)}

for (spherical) coordinates
\left(\alpha_{1}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}}),\ldots,\alpha_{s}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
parameterizing the unit hypersphere over
\bm{\mathsf{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{s-1}
.
We then solve the constrained optimization problem

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{\theta}}^{*}=\arg\min_{\bm{\mathsf{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{s-1}}\frac{\max\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}\left|\left(\alpha_{1}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(i)}+\ldots+\alpha_{s}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{s}^{(i)}\right)^{*}\bm{\mathsf{t}}^{(j)}\right|}{\min\limits_{j\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}\left|\left(\alpha_{1}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(i)}+\ldots+\alpha_{s}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{s}^{(i)}\right)^{*}\bm{\mathsf{t}}^{(j)}\right|},

to write our desired null space vector as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{z}}^{(i)}=\alpha_{1}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}}^{*})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{(i)}+\ldots+\alpha_{s}(\bm{\mathsf{\theta}}^{*})\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{s}^{(i)},

and compute the basis matrix
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}
as in Section
4
.

The overall computational cost of this optimization procedure is negligible when it is integrated into Algorithm
2
, since the optimization happens over a convex region with generally no more than a 3-dimensional parameterization in practice.
The issue of greater concern is that each additional column in the tagging matrix corresponds to
k+p
additional matvecs with
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}
for
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
, though the total is still far fewer matvecs than in block nullification which we will verify numerically in Section
7
.
First, for completeness, we outline in the next section our method of reconstructing the full uniform BLR representation of (
6
).

6

Randomized Compression Algorithms for Uniform BLR Matrices

In the previous sections, we focused on step
(I)
of randomized compression of uniform BLR matrices—the computation of basis matrices
\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}
from random sketches
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}
.
This was, in large part, due to the similarity of compression algorithms after basis matrices have been computed.
More precisely, steps
(II)
and
(III)
of uniform BLR compression can be executed in a manner that is oblivious to the particular algorithm used to compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
in step
(I)
, allowing for direct performance comparisons of Algorithms
1
and
2
.

In this section, we briefly describe how steps
(I)
-
(III)
of uniform BLR compression are conducted in our experiments.
Remark
6.1
also summarizes the compression procedure when matrix entries are readily available.
First, though, we present in Algorithm
3
the last basis construction algorithm that we consider as a benchmark for step
(I)
, which is equivalent to a blocked version of the randomized SVD done “naively” with
O(bk)
structured Gaussian test matrices.
We then describe a uniform BLR compression procedure that can be performed with basis matrices obtained from any of Algorithms
1
-
3
; we discuss more involved compression algorithms that reuse the sketches from step
(I)
for steps
(II)
and
(III)
in the Appendix.

Remark 6.1

When matrix entries are readily available, the task of recovering a uniform BLR representation as in (
6
) can be done with
k+p
matvecs of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{D}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}-\bm{\mathsf{D}}^{*}
, where
\bm{\mathsf{D}}
is the near-neighbor matrix given by
\bm{\mathsf{D}}_{i,j}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}_{i,j}
, for
i=1,\ldots,b
and
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
. The recovery of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}
is then done by accessing
O(bk)
matrix entries if
\bm{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
are computed as interpolative bases; see
[
41
, Chapter 18]
for more details. Note that Sections
3
and
4
are still applicable for the basis computations of step
(I)
in this instance.

Algorithm 3

Naive RandSVD for Basis Construction

1:

Fast matrix-vector multiplication with uniform BLR
\bm{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
and
\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
,
b\times b
flat matrix tessellation, maximum block-size
m
,
d
-dimensional geometry

2:

\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times bk}
in uniform BLR representation of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}
as in (
6
)

3:

Set
r=k+p

4:

for
blocks
i=1,\ldots,b

do

5:

Draw independent Gaussian test matrices
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}},\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times r}

6:

Set
\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}(I_{j},:)=0
and
\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}(I_{j},:)=0
for all
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

7:

Form
\bm{\mathsf{Y}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{Z}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}^{*}\bm{\mathsf{\Psi}}

8:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{i}=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{Y}}(I_{i},:),\ k\right)

9:

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{i}=\texttt{col}\left(\bm{\mathsf{Z}}(I_{i},:),\ k\right)

10:

end

for

11:

Set
\bm{\mathsf{U}}=\textrm{diag}\left(\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{U}}_{b}\right)
and
\bm{\mathsf{V}}=\textrm{diag}\left(\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{V}}_{b}\right)

6.1

Direct evaluation in steps
(II)
and
(III)

We present algorithms to compress uniform BLR matrices that allow for the most direct comparison of our basis construction algorithms.
First, we summarize in Algorithm
3
the last basis construction algorithm that we consider only as a benchmark for step
(I)
, which is equivalent to a blocked version of the randomized SVD done “naively” with
O(bk)
structured Gaussian test matrices.

The most straightforward way to accomplish steps
(II)
and
(III)
for uniform BLR matrices (
6
) is to evaluate
\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}(\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{V}})
directly, for
bk
additional matrix-vector multiplications (matvecs) with the basis matrix
\bm{\mathsf{V}}
from step
(I)
.
Once
\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}
has been computed,
3^{d}
sparse structured matrices of size
N\times m
, containing the
m\times m
identity as a submatrix, can be used to extract the nonzero entries of
\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*}
to obtain
\bm{\mathsf{B}}
, e.g.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccc}\bm{\mathsf{Y}}_{\bm{\mathsf{B}}}&=&(\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*})&\bm{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\bm{\mathsf{B}}}\\

-
3
, these reconstruction algorithms are summarized in Table
1
, which we say are of type A; this is in contrast to type B and type C algorithms, which are covered in the appendix.
Note that steps 2-3 in Table
1
are identical for all type A algorithms, making them the most ideal for performance comparisons of our basis reconstruction algorithms.

1.

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}
with block nullification (Algorithm
1
)

2.

Form
\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}(\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{V}})

3.

Form
\bm{\mathsf{B}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*}
with sparse structured identity matrices

1.

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}
with tagging (Algorithm
2
)

2.

Form
\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}(\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{V}})

3.

Form
\bm{\mathsf{B}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*}
with sparse structured identity matrices

Algorithm A3:

1.

Compute
\bm{\mathsf{U}},\bm{\mathsf{V}}
with naive randSVD (Algorithm
3
)

2.

Form
\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}=\bm{\mathsf{U}}^{*}(\bm{\mathsf{A}}\bm{\mathsf{V}})

3.

Form
\bm{\mathsf{B}}=\bm{\mathsf{A}}-\bm{\mathsf{U}}\widetilde{\bm{\mathsf{A}}}\bm{\mathsf{V}}^{*}
with sparse structured identity matrices
